TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

Have feedback? Hit reply to let me know your thoughts. Southeast Asia’s Youth: Underemployed and Angry In the third quarter of this year, Vietnam grew at a breakneck pace of 9.95 percent. At the same time, many worry about a social phenomenon called three không , or three no’s: no job, no education, and no training. According to third-quarter figures, the number of people aged 15-24 in this category stands at 1.6 million, which is an increase of 195,000. This is 11.2 percent of Vietnam’s youth. Such a high level of youth unemployment in the region’s fastest-growing economy is striking. In many other economies in the region, the levels are even higher, according to 2025 figures from the International Labour Organization. Indonesia sits at 21.5 percent, the highest of any major economy in the region. Thailand is at 12.9 percent, the Philippines is at 12.7%, and even strongly growing Malaysia is at 10.3 percent. In the region, only Singapore has a rate below 10 percent. Zoom out and the picture looks a bit rosier. The figures are actually below pre-pandemic levels. Zoom further in, however, and the picture looks grimmer. Across the region, young people are more and more likely to have a university degree. But many of the jobs being created are not good ones. In Indonesia, this phenomenon is particularly pronounced. Not only are most new jobs in lower-productivity sectors like agriculture and restaurants, but informal employment is absorbing a growing share of educated workers, according to research by the World Bank. In Thailand, the creation of high-quality jobs has declined since 2021, with employment growth driven by employment in lower-productivity sectors. Workers have even started leave cities, returning to family farms. Even Malaysia’s relatively strong growth in high-skill jobs has failed to keep pace with the number of people with tertiary education. * Why Party Plenums Matter So Much to Xi
  Foreign Policy | 27 Oct 21:28 UTC
  The Chinese leader is deeply reliant on formal mechanisms of power.
  FULL TEXT: Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s China Brief. The highlights this week: The Chinese Communist Party begins its fifth plenum , North Korean leader Kim Jong Un visits Dandong, and China’s first entirely AI-generated animated feature flops at the box office. Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s China Brief. The highlights this week: The Chinese Communist Party begins its fifth plenum , North Korean leader Kim Jong Un visits Dandong, and China’s first entirely AI-generated animated feature flops at the box office. The Chinese Communist Party (CCP) kicked off its fifth plenum on Monday. Plenums operate on a five-year cycle, bringing together the CCP’s Central Committee, a body of roughly 200 voting members and about 170 nonvoting alternates. The group includes Chinese President Xi Jinping, military leaders, department heads, regional leaders, and the mayors and party secretaries of major cities. As with every CCP leadership meeting, smaller groups tend to informally settle agendas and outcomes in advance, and discussion is pro forma. There are exceptions, such as former President Hu Jintao’s humiliating removal from the party congress in 2022 and senior official Bo Xilai’s expulsion in 2012, but they are rare. The agenda itself offers clues to Xi’s concerns. Plenums follow a regular cycle; fifth plenums have traditionally focused on the management of China’s semi-planned economy. This week’s agenda is instead centered on “party self-governance,” a formulation that encompasses both anti-corruption efforts and political loyalty to Xi. That shift suggests that Xi might be worried that his long-running anti-corruption campaigns and purges are not paying off. When he took office in 2012, Xi was empowered by a consensus among senior leaders that corruption and slackening party control were serious threats to the CCP’s legitimacy. Yet the generation of leaders that he has elevated to replace the corrupt leadership that preceded him has turned out to be just as corrupt. Plenums especially matter to Xi because his power is rooted in the CCP’s formal structures. This stands in contrast to Mao Zedong, who bypassed formal structures to attack his enemies and strengthen his personality cult. Mao did so most brutally during the Cultural Revolution, when he mobilized the student-led Red Guards against the CCP’s established leaders and hierarchies. Mao’s successor, Hua Guofeng, also tried to build a personality cult, but Deng Xiaoping eventually pushed him aside. Deng also operated outsid
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/27/china-ccp-fifth-plenum-xi-jinping-politics-corruption/

U.S. President Donald Trump is facing yet another court challenge to his tariff-driven war on trade, after an unbroken string of prior defeats that leave the future of his mercurial trade policy increasingly uncertain. Last week, the same company that successfully challenged Trump’s global tariffs under the 1977 International Emergency Economic Powers Act filed suit against his latest attempt to reach back in time. At issue is Trump’s use of an obscure and never-used statute, Section 338 of the 1930 Trade Act, to punish Canada for its allegedly discriminatory behavior toward U.S. commerce. This new trade fight may not be as clear-cut as some of the prior Trump defeats in court, though. The central thrust of the lawsuit challenging Sec. 338—part of the notorious Smoot-Hawley law that ushered in the Great Depression—is that the law was explicitly designed for a different world. The statute, which had never been used and in fact was unknown even to many trade experts until Trump resurrected it, was designed to give the United States a way to push back against countries such as France and Great Britain that were discriminating against U.S. exports as part of “imperial preference”—a circling of the wagons that gave preferential treatment to colonies at the expense of global trade in the dawning days of the depression. The statute was “all about imperial preferences,” said Sara Albrecht, the chairman and chief executive of the Liberty Justice Center, which filed the lawsuit on behalf of a U.S. business harmed by Trump’s latest trade spat. And even more clearly, the statute was meant to give the White House a tool to deter other countries from discriminating against U
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/27/trump-tariffs-trade-war-court-ieepa/

These events highlight an increasingly visible problem of space exploration: Satellite mega-constellations aren’t only transforming our night skies. Humanity’s expansion into space is beginning to reproduce the same take-make-waste model that has caused significant environmental damage on Earth. The number of objects in Earth’s orbit has risen dramatically and is likely to increase several-fold again over the coming decade. In 2010, the active satellite population was roughly 1,000. Today, space surveillance networks track approximately 40,000 objects in the Earth’s orbit. But only around 17,000 of these are still active payloads; the rest are retired satellites, spent rocket parts, and other fragments. When it comes to smaller space debris, the European Space Agency (ESA) estimates that more than 1.2 million debris fragments larger than 1 centimeter are already orbiting Earth. The greatest danger posed by these smaller fragments is that they will collide with functioning spacecraft. Traveling at several kilometers per s

* China Is Building Its Way Around Europe’s EV Tariffs
  Foreign Policy | 27 Oct 09:00 UTC
  Beijing’s evolving industrial playbook is taking shape in Morocco.
  FULL TEXT: In recent years, Europe’s auto industry has reeled from the influx of low-cost, high-quality Chinese electric vehicles (EVs). Even as Brussels imposes tariffs on Chinese-made EVs, their market share only continues to grow. As of December, more than 9 percent of all new automobiles sold in Europe were made in China, with some estimates expecting Chinese EVs to account for roughly 20 percent of the Western European market by 2028. Brussels’s retaliatory tariffs reflect a common Western view that high-value Chinese products are competitive in global markets largely because of state subsidies . To be sure, China’s EV industry has benefited immensely from Beijing’s subsidies, easy credit, and tax relief. That explanation, however, is far too narrow and risks severely underestimating the scale of the challenge ahead. In recent years, Europe’s auto industry has reeled from the influx of low-cost, high-quality Chinese electric vehicles (EVs). Even as Brussels imposes tariffs on Chinese-made EVs, their market share only continues to grow. As of December, more than 9 percent of all new automobiles sold in Europe were made in China, with some estimates expecting Chinese EVs to account for roughly 20 percent of the Western European market by 2028. Brussels’s retaliatory tariffs reflect a common Western view that high-value Chinese products are competitive in global markets largely because of state subsidies . To be sure, China’s EV industry has benefited immensely from Beijing’s subsidies, easy credit, and tax relief. That explanation, however, is far too narrow and risks severely underestimating the scale of the challenge ahead. The more consequential story—that will likely make Chinese EVs even more competitive in the near future—is Beijing’s evolving approach to overseas industrial policy. At the core of this approach is an increasingly coordinated model of public- and private-sector activity. State-owned enterprises (SOE) lay the groundwork for favorable investment environments abroad by undertaking massive infrastructure projects and securing access to strategic resources. Boosted by Beijing’s diplomatic ties and international clout, private companies leverage these state-backed platforms as a springboard for trade by bringing in investment, production capacity, and industry-specific expertise. The advantages of this approach for Beijing—and the challenge that it poses to foreign companies—are already on full display in Morocco, where years of existing strategic 
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/27/china-europe-morocco-electric-vehicle-manufacturing-industrial-policy/

* Q&A: Why are UK energy bills so high – and how could they be lowered?
  Carbon Brief | 27 Oct 16:47 UTC
  Energy bills in the UK have continued to soar in 2026, with intense debate raging about how to bring them… The post Q&A: Why are UK energy bills so high – and how could they be lowered? appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: Energy bills in the UK have continued to soar in 2026, with intense debate raging about how to bring them down. In October 2026, the cap on household energy bills reached its highest level in three years, rising by £60 amid high gas prices driven by the Iran war. The cap is forecast to jump by as much as another quarter in January 2027, pushing annual energy bills towards £2,200 . Energy bills have not been that high since 2022 when, in the fallout of the Russian invasion of Ukraine, the UK government spent more than £60bn on supporting consumers through the crisis. While expensive gas is behind the lion’s share of the rise in bills since 2020, policy costs and investment in the gas and electricity grids are also putting some pressure on prices. This has sparked conflicting calls to continue the clean-energy transition to get off the “ fossil-fuel rollercoaster ”, to move more slowly – or to abandon climate goals in favour of more gas. Below, Carbon Brief looks at the real drivers of high energy bills in the UK, what has been done to address them to date and what options are available to cut them going forward. These include taking more policy costs off electricity bills, boosting flexibility or shifting to more efficient technologies with lower running costs, such as heat pumps and electric vehicles (EVs). In October 2026, UK household energy bills reached their highest levels since 2022, with the price cap for typical homes hitting £1,723 per year, up 47% in real terms since October 2020. Industrial energy users also face high costs, particularly for their electricity . For households, another sharp increase is now all but guaranteed under the price cap for January 2027, which is forecast to rise by around 25% from current levels. Wholesale costs for electricity and gas make up 44% of the current cap of £1,723 per year, according to Carbon Brief analysis of figures from thinktank Nesta , shown in the figure below. The wholesale cost of both electricity and gas is tied to gas prices, due to the marginal pricing system used in power markets in the UK and most other countries. Network charges make up a further 24% of bills and operating costs are another 17%, whereas policy costs only account for 7% of the total. There has been increasing interest in the so-called “ non-commodity costs ” – those not related to wholesale energy – that now make up a significant portion of bills, as shown in the figure above. Yet while these costs have been increasing – partic
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/qa-why-are-uk-energy-bills-so-high-and-how-could-they-be-lowered

* Guest post: Greenland ice sheet shrinks for 30th year in a row
  Carbon Brief | 27 Oct 14:07 UTC
  Greenland has now marked the 30th consecutive year where it lost more ice than it gained. Over the 12-month period… The post Guest post: Greenland ice sheet shrinks for 30th year in a row appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: Greenland has now marked the 30th consecutive year where it lost more ice than it gained. Dr Martin Stendel , climate scientist at the Danish Meteorological Institute (DMI), part of the Polar Portal Prof Ruth Mottram , climate scientist at DMI and honorary professor at Aarhus University Over the 12-month period to August 2026, the world’s second-largest ice sheet lost around 135bn tonnes of ice. This major ice loss came as the region experienced a relatively cool start to summer and less surface melt than in previous years. In other words, it was not an “extreme” year for weather in Greenland – and, yet, the ice sheet ended firmly in the red. Three decades of continuous ice loss in Greenland is significant, given that scientists typically need 30 years of data to separate the “ fingerprint ” of human-caused climate change from natural variations in the climate. In Greenland, the direction of travel has been the same – downward – every single year for 30 years. These latest figures come as a new report highlights that ice melting processes seen for decades in Greenland are increasingly being replicated in Antarctica. Greenland, therefore, is not only the largest present-day source of ice-driven sea level rise – but offers a preview of what is in store for the world’s largest ice sheet. (For our previous analyses of Greenland’s ice cover, see coverage in 2025 , 2024 , 2023 , 2022 , 2021 , 2020 , 2019 , 2018 , 2017 , 2016 and 2015 .) One way that scientists track the health of the Greenland ice sheet is by tracking changes to its “surface mass balance”. This is the difference between ice gains from snowfall and losses from melt at the surface of Greenland’s ice sheet. Greenland’s annual ice cycle runs from 1 September through to the end of August. The surface mass balance at the end of this period has, to date, always been positive, because the surface accumulates more snow than it loses from melting ice. The ice sheet typically gains mass during the colder months of autumn, winter and spring. Then, as temperatures increase from around mid-June, the ice sheet begins to lose more ice through surface melt than it gains from snow. (Greenland also loses ice when icebergs break off – or “calve” – into the sea, as well when the front of glaciers melt when they meet warm sea water. These losses are included in the “ total mass balance ” – see below.) Greenland ended 2025-26 with an overall surface mass balance of 519bn tonnes. This is the sixth highest in a dataset 
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/guest-post-greenland-ice-sheet-shrinks-for-30th-year-in-a-row

* Intel, Oracle, IBM Join Objections to OKX-ICE Tokenized Stock Venue, Bringing Total to 26
  Decrypt | 27 Oct 18:46 UTC [also carried by: CoinDesk]
  Intel, Oracle, IBM, and IonQ became the latest companies to object to trading of their shares on the planned OKX-ICE tokenized stock venue, bringing the total to 26.
  FULL TEXT: The tech sector’s resistance to tokenized stocks keeps building. Intel, Oracle, IBM, and quantum computing firm IonQ are the latest companies to object to having their shares traded on OKXICE TSV, according to an updated public notice from the venue dated Monday. The planned platform, a 50-50 joint venture between NYSE owner Intercontinental Exchange and OKX’s U.S. holding company, now lists 26 objecting issuers. That’s up from 22 in its Oct. 23 notice, which added Wells Fargo , Cisco, and Marvell. The tally stood at 16 on Oct. 19 and nine on Oct. 15, when the first batch included Verizon, Broadcom and UnitedHealth Group . In less than two weeks, the list has nearly tripled. Technology companies dominate the roster. Microsoft, Alphabet, Broadcom , Qualcomm, Arm, Cisco, and now Intel and Oracle have all objected, along with banks Goldman Sachs and Wells Fargo. IBM and IonQ add two quantum computing players. As before, the notices don’t explain the companies’ reasons or what an objection means for whether a stock can trade on the platform. OKXICE TSV plans to let approved users trade tokenized U.S. stocks 24/7 through permissioned Uniswap v4 liquidity pools on OKX’s XLayer blockchain, paired with stablecoins such as USDC. Users must pass identity and sanctions checks and hold a nontransferable “soulbound” token to trade. The venue is set to operate under the SEC’s innovation exemption, which the agency unveiled in September after the Clarity Act stalled in the Senate. The latest notice highlights a key risk for investors. Each token represents a claim on a real share held by a third-party “tokenizer” through a registered broker-dealer. If that broker-dealer failed, the notice says, it’s uncertain whether tokenized shares in a self-custodied wallet would count as customer property or be covered by the Securities Investor Protection Corporation, which protects brokerage customers when firms fail. The notice also says trades must be fully funded, with no margin or borrowing. Liquidity providers don’t have to be registered market makers and can pull their liquidity at any time. OKX and ICE first teamed up on tokenized securities earlier this year before unveiling plans for the venue this month. Start every day with the top news stories right now, plus original features, a podcast, videos and more.
  https://decrypt.co/381998/intel-oracle-ibm-join-objections-okx-ice-tokenized-stock-total-26

* Morning Minute: Tokenized Stock Battle Looms Over Robinhood’s Earnings Call
  Decrypt | 27 Oct 12:59 UTC
  One of crypto’s primary growth sectors for this upcoming cycle is under attack. Will Vlad Tenev speak up on today’s Robinhood earnings call?
  FULL TEXT: Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner . The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And c heck out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action. Somebody bought a full-page ad in the Wall Street Journal this weekend to tell public company CEOs that crypto is coming for their shares. “YOUR STOCK. THEIR TOKENS. YOUR PROBLEM,” it read, paid for by the Common Sense Leadership Fund, a conservative nonprofit. They listed eight risks - pricing disparities, obscured ownership, impaired capital raising, cybertheft, market manipulation, AML and KYC loopholes, absent investor protections, legal liability. And the call to action was blunt: the SEC has given you the right to opt out, you have 30 days, this is your only chance. The war against tokenized stocks is brewing. Nine companies objected to having their shares tokenized on the OKX-ICE venue on October 15. By October 19 it was 16. By October 23, the list hit 22 , including Wells Fargo, Cisco, Marvell, Arm, Moderna and Intuit joining Microsoft, Alphabet, Goldman Sachs, Verizon, Broadcom and UnitedHealth. That’s a two-and-a-half-fold increase in eight days, and 35% of the 63 companies the venue planned to list. OKXICE even amended its rules on October 19 to let issuers tokenize their own shares, which would hand them control. Not one objector has taken it. They don’t want a better version of the product. They don’t want it. Robinhood reports third-quarter results after the bell, and no company has more riding on how this fight resolves. Robinhood Chain launched its public mainnet on July 1. In its debut quarter it generated roughly $51 million in revenue. That’s more than Ethereum’s $41 million, while Ethereum’s own network revenue fell 35%. It processed about $65 billion in DEX volume, fourth among all networks. Of that, $13.7 billion was tokenized equities. One fifth of everything trading on the chain, in a single quarter, from a zero start. Robinhood’s stock tokens have already drawn fire (AMC’s CEO called them “contemptible”) and the company is building an entire ecosystem on top of them. Long.xyz pairs memecoins against tokenized NVDA and TSLA, and meme-versus-stock pairs now drive most stock-token volume on the chain. Orbio funds AI inference credits with creator fees paid in tokenized NVDA. Pull the stock tokens out, and a lot of that architecture has nothing to stand on. So will Vlad Tenev addres
  https://decrypt.co/381907/morning-minute-tokenized-stock-battle-looms-over-robinhoods-earnings-call

This profile explores Morrison’s perseverance and shows how, for some people, a life defined by extreme risk can never be retreated from. “It was a profound, transformational moment for me to know that when I found Hilaree, she wasn’t in her body,” Morrison told me. “I dug her out of the snow. I had been haunted so much by my wife and my children, and what state they were in when the plane crashed, and how they were recovered. I had been haunted by these visions of their half-burned corpses. Then contrast that with what I went through with Hilaree. There was so much more finality to it.” Morrison had her body cremated in Kathmandu, at a Buddhist temple. “Did an experienced alpinist leave his girlfriend to die at the top of a mountain?” “On May 22, a 7-year-old reached the summit of El Capitan.   FULL TEXT: In 1993, an audience member at the Citizen Cup tennis tournament stabbed Monica Seles, then a 19-year-old phenom who was then dethroned from the top of the world tennis rankings. It deserves your deeper consideration. Perhaps the most profound shift is the collapse of our ability to trust what we see. In 1993, we had the tape. We saw Parche reach over the rail and thrust his blade into Seles’ back. We agreed on the wound. Today, that on-the-ground truth is being eroded.   The would-be democrats of Serbia’s Student List find themselves in a complex situation: Their aspirations to remake the country’s political order depend, in part, on the support of some of the region’s most reactionary elements. The post Serbia’s Victorious New Democrats Face an Old Question appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: For the second time in just over a quarter-century, Serbia has seemingly thrown off the yoke of one-man rule. In October 2000, Serbians mobilized — first at the ballot box, then in the streets — to oust the genocidal regime of Slobodan Milošević . In October 2026, it was sufficient for them merely to vote in overwhelming numbers to force the concession of the strongman at the helm. This time Serbians ousted Milošević’s protege, Aleksandar Vučić, and his ruling Serbian Progressive Party (SNS) after their nearly 14-year tenure in government. The vote capped two years of protest that began in November 2024, when a newly renovated canopy at Novi Sad’s railway station collapsed, killing 16 people. An insurgent student movement blamed the deaths on government corruption, which they saw as a hallmark of Vučić’s rule, and their ensuing blockades of universities grew into the largest protest movement in Serbia since 2000. The movement eventually coalesced into the so-called Student List parliamentary coalition. On Sunday, this bloc won 53% of the popular vote and secured around 140 seats in the country’s 250-seat National Assembly, providing it with a comfortable, stable governing majority. Domestically, the Student List ran on a sweeping anti-corruption platform. Its foreign policy agenda, however, is virtually indistinguishable from that of the SNS — at least on paper. Why? Because Serbian nationalism remains, arguably, the most potent political force in the country. The vote that propelled the Serbian List into power is a heterodox amalgam of the country’s moderate, liberal public and the antiregime far right (voters who believe Vučić has been insufficiently hard-line, for instance, in opposing Kosovo’s sovereignty, and would like the country to pursue still deeper ties with Moscow). The same curious combination was evident throughout the two-year protest movement preceding the vote. A very similar coalition toppled Milošević in 2000. This is the bind in which Serbia’s would-be democrats find themselves. Their aspirations to remake the country’s political order depend, in part, on the support of some of the region’s most reactionary elements. Yet the past 40 years suggest that, much as in Russia, Serbia’s regional ambitions and democratization at home are at odds. The reason is institutional.   FULL TEXT: Editor’s Note: Passages is a members-only monthly newsletter about life-changing encounters with stories. There’s some nonfiction in there: Into the Ice by Mark Synnott, Boom by Michael Shnayerson, and the audiobook of Entitled by Andrew Lownie, which, if you need to be convinced that Prince Andrew is one of the most odious people on the planet, boy do I have the book for you. I hate titles that are unmemorable strings of nonspecific words!) I’m in a race to finish Venetian Vespers by John Banville before Libby, the public library app, takes it back for maybe the fourth time. The Banville is short and enjoyable but has been a victim of the smorgasbord approach, never quite seizing priority. Also on my Libby shelf are, among others, Lovers XXX by Allie Rowbottom, The Original by Nell Stevens, and Villa Coco by Andrew Sean Greer. On my Bookshop.org app, I have The Remembered Soldier , a magnificent novel by the Dutch writer Anjet Daanje that is very long and that I ended up (happily) buying because I didn’t want to race Libby’s loan period. I wasn’t an early adopter of e-books, partly because my first novel was published in 2012, at the height of e-book doomerism, when everyone was saying print books were soon to be a thing of the past and e-books were the enemy. * Love with nowhere to go
  Aeon | 27 Oct 10:00 UTC
  Grief is not only for the dead. When we are unable to honour other profound losses, we become strangers to ourselves - by Derek van Zoonen Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Telemark, Norway, 2011. Photo by Jonas Bendiksen/Magnum Photos I have been grieving my mother for years, even though she is still alive. Sometimes before falling asleep – in that liminal space between waking and sleeping where the self becomes porous – I’d see the image of the sweet and caring woman who would come to say goodnight, her cheeks covered in thick layers of night cream. Then, out of nowhere, the image would flip into the raging woman who was so entangled in her own suffering that she turned her frustration on us. Over the years, that second woman slowly swallowed the first, as she got pulled into my brother’s addiction. It’s hard to say where her own unhappiness ended and my brother’s suffering began, but together they drifted into a parallel reality, cut off from the world beyond their heads. There was no funeral for this. No condolence cards. No flowers. No phone calls. No leave of absence. What was I supposed to mourn? And yet what I felt for them, what I still feel, has the unmistakable gravity of mourning. Can there be grief without death? I have come to think that we grieve much more often than we realise, and that death is grief’s most visible occasion rather than its essence. If that doesn’t match your experience, it may be because we rarely call these other losses by their name. Most of our losses arrive without a funeral. That is the human predicament: we are creatures built to attach, living in a world where nothing stays. Sooner or later, we lose the things we care about. And since we are complex beings with a body, a mind, a community and an environment – beings who love many different things – there is a great deal we can lose, from our physical health to our sense of who we are, from relationships to homelands to the natural world we share with other species. For creatures like us, there is no way around heartbreak: to live is to lose. Grief is what love does when its object is gone. It is love with nowhere to go. Over the past few years I have accumulated many such losses. There is the psychological loss of my father as I knew and loved him: a brutal stroke rendered him incapable of walking and cycling, and cycling was the only thing that could ‘make his thoughts liquid’, as he used to say. There is the loss of old friendships. First you are no longer part of each other’s lives – you just update one another about your life – and then, one day, it becomes awkward to reach out at all. There is the gradual softening of an academic 
  https://aeon.co/essays/we-need-a-language-to-name-the-grief-that-comes-without-death

* AI models cost more when you don’t use English
  Rest of World | 26 Oct 10:00 UTC
  Some languages require far more tokens for AI models to process, raising costs and reducing context for the people who use them.
  FULL TEXT: People who use less widely spoken languages could end up paying more to use artificial intelligence than those who speak English or other dominant languages. A new study published last month by the Coalition for Language Equity in AI Research found AI models require far more tokens for some languages than others. Tokens are the units of text or data that the models process. The researchers have called this a hidden “tokenizer tax,” which can make AI more expensive in languages with smaller online presence. A tokenizer is a tool that breaks down raw text, images, or audio into tokens. Even within the 20 most popular languages on the internet, there is a gap in token usage, the research found. The most expensive of the languages used between 1.5 and 5 times more tokens than the cheapest. This gap widened when researchers expanded the set to 200 languages, with the token cost gap in one AI model growing by 17 times. “A startup in [Bengaluru] may pay a surcharge on every prompt and completion while its competitor in Austin does not,” the study said. “The tokenizer tax also has implications beyond cost. Higher token counts translate into higher latency and reduced effective context capacity. These penalties hit hardest where compute and connectivity are already limited. Tokenizer inequity is a constraint on who can participate in the AI economy.” Non-English languages leave less room for documents or conversation history, and require more work to generate the answer, according to Pratyush Choudhury, co-founder of Activate AI, India’s first venture capital fund dedicated exclusively to AI. Activate backs multilingual voice AI startups like ElevenLabs, Sarvam, and Wispr Flow. “Think about a tutor: Retaining the student’s earlier mistakes and explanations is part of delivering a good lesson. If that history consumes the context window much faster in one language, the disadvantage reaches into the quality of the service,” he said. One way to improve language processing is to improve the underlying data. “Open data efforts matter here, like RESPIN, a collaborative initiative with [the Indian Institute of Science] funded by the Gates Foundation, where we collected text from native speakers of 38 dialects along with read speech,” Raoul Nanavati, co-founder and CEO of Mumbai-based AI voice infrastructure startup Navana.ai , told Rest of World .   https://restofworld.org/2026/ai-tokenizer-tax-non-english-languages-cost-inequity/?utm_source=rss&utm_medium=rss&utm_campaign=feeds

* A Change at a Damascus School Has Syrians Reading the Runes
  New Lines | 23 Oct 10:24 UTC
  A hundred years after its founding, the elite, secular Laique school in Damascus has decided to separate boys and girls for the first time. Syrians are asking what that means for the school, and the future of Syria. The post A Change at a Damascus School Has Syrians Reading the Runes appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: Exactly 100 years ago, a secular French school opened in Damascus with 79 students, in rented premises close to where the current parliament stands on Abid Street. The school was known then as the Laique Institute and is known today as the Al-Hurriya Institute, but is generally referred to as just Laique. Four years later, in 1930, then-Prime Minister Taj al-Din al-Hasani, a turbaned cleric with a reformist agenda, granted the school a plot of land on Baghdad Street, to which it formally relocated in 1932. Laique quickly became one of the most prestigious institutions in Syria, celebrated for its coeducation, the quality of its faculty, and its tennis, volleyball and basketball courts. It also had an impressive library, along with a full-fledged theater that once hosted Egyptian diva Umm Kulthum in 1955. That colorful tapestry was part of a Middle East that imagined itself as part of a broader Arab world with a united identity. Fast forward 100 years, and Laique has become a shadow of its former self. First came 61 years of Baath Party rule that imposed an indoctrinating socialist curriculum on the entire Syrian school system, along with khaki uniforms. Teachers at Laique were hired not because they were good, but because they were Baathists. Then came the 1967 nationalization that ended the school’s autonomy and distorted its institutional identity, renaming it “Mahad al-Huriyya,” or the “Institute of Freedom,” a nod to the language of Baathism, with its three ideological pillars of freedom, pan-Arabism and socialism. The fact that Hafez al-Assad chose Laique for all his children — Bashar included — only hardened the school’s Baathism. When Assad’s eldest son and chosen successor, Bassel al-Assad, died in 1994, the school was renamed after him. What did not disappear was the memory of what Laique had been, and, more importantly, its alumni network of engineers, doctors, politicians and artists who became the “who’s who” of Syrian society. For decades, “I went to Laique” functioned as a kind of passport, akin to “I went to Brummana High School” in Lebanon: a signal of academic excellence that guaranteed entry into the workforce and came with high expectations of talent and character. A great deal of Syrian history was compressed into a single address. This was a school founded under the French mandate, legitimized by a reformist cleric premier, staffed by French and Syrian teachers, and attended by the children of the urban bourgeoisie. * Traction!
  Aeon | 23 Oct 10:00 UTC
  Tribologists study friction: what happens when things rub together. It’s indispensable, from the wheel to the paths of stars - by Jennifer R Vail Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: In conversations, few words catch my attention faster than ‘friction’ – and especially talk of making things frictionless . From the workplace to grocery shopping, our societies seem to be striving to remove friction from everything. The motivation appears to be that decreasing resistance makes life easier and smoother. Having spent a career as a tribologist, studying friction, wear and lubrication scientifically, I have some opinions on this. I’ll confess that, in most of my career, I too have sought to reduce friction, developing new materials, designs or even instrumentation in order to lower friction in a jet engine or reduce emissions from your car. However, I’ve also found myself trying to figure out how to increase friction to remove plaque on teeth. The reality is that friction, despite seeming so antagonistic, is necessary and even productive. The force that resists motion has, since humans first struck a spark from a stone, been the very thing that propels us forward. And this paradox offers a lens through which to reread humanity’s entire technological history. I n order to appreciate all that an understanding of friction has helped us to accomplish, it’s necessary to start at square one and state what friction actually is. We define friction as the force that resists motion, and that means friction is rather ubiquitous. If things are moving, whether they’re sliding around or rolling, there’s friction. Solids, liquids, gases? Doesn’t matter. If there’s movement, friction is there to resist it. Unfortunately, because friction’s job is to resist, we’ve developed an aversion to it as a nuisance. But it’s a very important force, and we’ve thrived by expanding our understanding of it. By the 1960s, after millennia of evolving with friction, we finally accepted that friction is such a critical part of our world that a scientific field of study needed to be devoted to it: tribology. Tribologists like me study this force in all its forms. Tribology is the science of interacting surfaces in relative motion, concerned with the wear of materials, friction and lubrication. Basically, tribology is the specialisation of things rubbing against each other. You may raise an eyebrow at a field of science focused on this, but when you pause to consider how much motion is going on inside us, around us and throughout the Universe, you can appreciate that tribology is vast. More than anyone, tribologists know the problems friction can cause. But they also know the ac
  https://aeon.co/essays/why-a-world-without-friction-is-a-world-without-progress

And then a half-blind monk, in the wake of a terrible plague, exhumed the victims and used them to redecorate. An ossuary is any repository for human remains, but it’s also the term for a holy site that serves this function. Another name for this kind of church is charnel house, which to me evokes the image of an abattoir.   FULL TEXT: After ChatGPT was released in November 2022, the tech industry was quick to herald the chatbot as the marker of a new wave of technological progress. As companies pushed to create more powerful AI models, the water demand of their ambitions took a while to become clear. In April 2023, a research paper out of the University of California estimated that training the large language model behind the initial version of ChatGPT had required 185,000 gallons of water. For every extended conversation someone had with the chatbot or every time they asked it to write an email, they were effectively pouring a bottle of water down the drain. That statistic got a lot of play when it was released. It became an early warning sign of what was coming as companies pushed chatbots as a universal technology that could replace psychologists, educators , lawyers, and even people’s friends or partners . As public concern grew, Google and OpenAI came out with their own figures. The companies suggested that querying one of their chatbots used mere drops of water. Independent researchers were not impressed, arguing the companies were taking a narrow view of the water use necessary to power and cool increasingly monstrous data centers. Those water demands can be quite significant too. A hyperscale data center can suck up between 1 and 5 million gallons of water a day, comparable to the amount used by a city of 10,000 to 50,000 people — depending on the part of the world we’re talking about. Consider how these companies often cluster data centers together and it becomes obvious what a challenge it can be to supply them. Data centers end up competing with communities over their potable water supply. The water demands of individual data centers are also continuing to grow. It’s not just that they need more water for cooling as they become larger and contain more servers. The graphics processing units (GPUs) that power generative AI are much more resource-intensive than the chips and storage devices that traditionally filled data center racks. GPUs run harder and throw off more heat , meaning they also need more energy or water to keep them cool. Data centers cannot just use any kind of water either; it has to be clean enough to ensure the equipment and pipes are not damaged. Tapping into public drinking water is both easier and cheaper than paying the cost of treatment themselves. That means data centers end up competing with communities over their potable water supply. Our little dyad, with dependants, was standing on its own feet, even if something felt decidedly unstable underneath.   Orthodox churches that have not shared communion since the fifth century are now worshipping together in North American cities, searching for a form of faith that transcends linguistic and cultural differences. The post The Basement Where Orthodoxy Is Being Rebuilt appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: Three people hold keys to St. Silouan the Athonite Orthodox Church in Toronto. One is the priest, one a deacon and the third is Anthony, who has been homeless for three years, lives with schizophrenia and serves as church warden. He opens up at 8 a.m. on Sundays and lights the candles before anyone arrives. “Technically, on the payroll, there’s me,” said the Rev. Paul Tadros, the rector. “And there’s a man who lives with schizophrenia,” referring to Anthony, who receives $200 a month. St. Silouan’s parish rents a basement from a Bulgarian Orthodox church in Regent Park, one of Canada’s most diverse neighborhoods. Around 80 people come on an average Sunday, including Greek, Romanian, Serbian, Ukrainian, Arab, Russian and Egyptian families, alongside Canadian-born converts. At 10 a.m., the lights come up on the hymn “Glory to Thee Who Has Shown Us The Light” and Samir Hatem, a Lebanese Maronite who grew up in an Oriental-rite church and never felt at home in Toronto’s Latin parishes, says that is the moment. “You feel that you entered into the light.” Hatem found St. Silouan’s on his own, by searching “Orthodox church Toronto,” and kept coming back once his wife, Roxana Aras, an anthropologist who studies Orthodox communities, started coming too. Then comes the Lord’s Prayer. Tadros switches his delivery from English into Greek, Slavonic, Romanian, Arabic, Coptic, Persian, Mandarin, Finnish, Cantonese and Hebrew. In total, it amounts to at least 16 languages. Only recently has anyone begun to think that it runs too long. “The many become one,” he said. “When I hear Mandarin or Farsi, these are not native languages to traditional Orthodox lands, but they are now.” There are few rooms on Earth where the same prayer is said in Arabic and Hebrew by people standing shoulder to shoulder. The languages spoken there predate most of today’s borders. Polina Sanfilippo, the choir director, was born in Moscow to a secular Russian Jewish family, moved to Israel at 7 and Canada at 16. She and her husband are godparents to a boy in the parish whose family is Egyptian. “Here I am, a Russian Jew, and my godson is Egyptian,” she said. “Where would that happen anywhere else?” What’s happening in that basement is happening, unevenly, everywhere Orthodoxy has landed across North America: The old model of one ethnicity, one language, one parish is buckling under the same pressures in different cities, and each community is answering differently. He implicated himself and his best friend, Forrest, along with two others, in a crime they did not commit. For Texas Monthly , Michael Hall covers the case and its horrific aftermath. Both boys were taken to a juvenile detention center, and at around midnight, Maurice was transferred to the police station. A couple of hours later, his questioning began, directed by Senior Sergeant Hector Polanco, a fifteen-year veteran with a reputation for getting confessions . Polanco was known to walk into an interrogation room and announce that his nickname was “El Diablo”; he would threaten the death penalty and grab a man’s arm and show just where the needle would go. Maurice was terrified. He later told his girlfriend, Kim, that Polanco had claimed that his pistol was the murder weapon—and showed him graphic photos of the dead girls. “They were throwing them in his face, saying, ‘This is how the girls look now,’ ” Kim told me. (Polanco later denied doing either of these things.) In a statement Maurice wrote later that afternoon, after several hours of questioning by Polanco, he admitted to having the gun the night of the murders but insisted he didn’t have anything to do with the crime. Instead, he claimed that Forrest had borrowed the gun, had been acting oddly (“I think he was on some kind of drugs. . . . He was jumping around and acting crazy”), and had boasted that he and a friend had shot the girls in the back of the store and started a fire with their bodies. * When it started to burn
  Aeon | 21 Oct 10:01 UTC
  Horrific violence, enduring terror and ceaseless solidarity: four years of war through the eyes of Ukrainian civilians - by Aeon Video Watch on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: The Norwegian filmmaker Anders Hammer is known for short films that capture global conflicts on the ground as they unfold. These include Do Not Split (2020), which follows the pro-democracy protests in Hong Kong, and The Takeover (2023), which chronicles the lives of Afghan women in the wake of the Taliban’s return to power. The third film in this trilogy, When It Started to Burn , documents Russia’s invasion of Ukraine over the course of four years, beginning in March 2022, as a combination of deep uncertainty and extraordinary resolve was setting in across the country. In particular, the film follows the life of a Kyiv native named Kseniia, who makes the difficult decision to send her children abroad as she stays in the country and runs a ‘humanitarian headquarters’ distributing donations to those in need. Through his portrait of the invasion from the perspective of Ukrainian civilians, Hammer explores how their lives are defined by horrific violence, enduring terror and ceaseless solidarity.
